## Secretariat's assessment of FP137

Proposal name:

Ghana Shea Landscape Emission Reductions Project

Accredited entity:

United Nations Development Programme (UNDP)

Country/(ies):

Ghana

Project/programme size:

Medium

## I. Overall assessment of the Secretariat

1. The funding proposal is presented to the Board for consideration with the following remarks:

| Strengths                                                                                                                                                                                                    | Points of caution                                                    |
|--------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------|----------------------------------------------------------------------|
| Strong community development, investment planning and creation of local development forest funds                                                                                                             | Securing strong buy-in from the shea market - meeting future demands |
| Has strong gender focus with significant level of women's engagement in the shea value chain                                                                                                                 |                                                                      |
| Opportunity to pilot an innovative restoration mechanism for shea parklands through performance-based agreements (Global Shea Alliance) with strong scalability to the entire Sahel zone for shea production |                                                                      |

2. The Board may wish to consider approving this funding proposal with the terms and conditions listed in the respective term sheet and addendum XVIII, titled 'List of proposed conditions and recommendations'.

## II. Summary of the Secretariat's assessment

## 2.1 Project background

3. The Ghana Shea Landscape Emission Reductions project (GSLERP) aims to restore degraded savannah forests and strengthen the livelihoods in the northern savannah zone (NSZ) through enhanced ecosystems services. The project seeks to enhance forest carbon stocks across the landscape in the NSZ through the following interventions:
2. (a) Restoring 200,000 hectares of off-reserve savannah forests/woodlands and placing them under self-financing community management in community resource management areas (CREMA);
3. (b) Restoring 100,000 hectares of degraded shea parklands;

<!-- image -->

- (c) Creating 25,500 hectares of modified taungya system (MTS) 1 /forest plantation in severely degraded forest reserves; and
- (d) Implementing an integrated monitoring system and completing the national REDD-plus architecture for safeguards, forest monitoring and reporting systems.
4. The project area is the NSZ - the northern reaches of the savannah zone - with an area of 9.7 million hectares, dominated by savannah forest and grassland, and which is also inhabited by a significant diversity of species that are only found in the savannah ecological zones. The forest ecosystem of the NSZ is characterized by nearly 400 million shea and other trees growing naturally in traditional farming systems. The area covers three administrative regions, namely the northern, upper east and upper west regions, and encompassing 61 per cent of the savannah zone in the country. With a population of over five million people, the NSZ region is the poorest region in the country, with poverty rates reaching over 80 per cent in many districts. With over 90 per cent of rural households engaged in rain-fed agricultural activities without alternative income opportunities, the low and highly varied rainfall patterns increase the vulnerability and lower agricultural productivity.
5. Shea landscapes are important sources of carbon storage and sequestration both above ground and in soils and roots. Shea has been retained as a semi-domesticated tree species, providing essential products and ecological services to this drier region. Shea production is beneficial for mitigation, but also improves adaptive capacities by making ecosystems more resilient and providing a buffer in ecosystem services through tree retention (e.g. water and soil retention, prevention of desertification). Shea, being a major source of economic livelihoods, for women in particular, provides critical economic support for the population in the region because the collection of shea fruits coincides with the 'lean' season of the year before the agricultural crops are harvested.
6. A significant increase in temperature and excessive droughts have been observed in the region, contributing to a decrease in soil fertility and soil water retention capacity. Transitions in the agriculture production system - from the traditional bush fallow agricultural system to use of mechanized ploughing approaches for certain crops (e.g. maize) - and facilitation of subsidized access to fertilizers have accelerated soil nutrient depletion, as well as removing trees and eliminating regeneration. Heavy reliance on wood for charcoal production in the region because of the low cost and ease of access to unmanaged forest resources in community lands also raises the need for sustainable fuel wood production from forest areas. Logging and mid-to-late dry season fires have also resulted in extensive forest degradation, which in turn increases the growth of grasses that are resulting in more intense fires in the dry season. It is estimated that 1,058,492 hectares have been deforested, resulting in emissions of 3,548,400 tCO2eq per year.
7. The total project finance needed is USD 54.55 million, with a request to GCF for grant finance of USD 30.1 million (55.2 per cent). The Forestry Commission is contributing USD 15 million in the form of grants (in-kind), and private sector companies are contributing a total of USD 9 million in the form of grants (in-kind). UNDP is also providing USD 0.35 million in the form of grants (in-kind). The environmental and social safeguards category of the project is B.

1 The MTS has evolved from the Taungya system that was practiced extensively between the 1960 and 1980s to rehabilitate degraded reserves. was developed based on the experiences of this system and introduced as part of

the National Forest Plantation Development Programme in 2002. By opening up additional land for agriculture albeit temporarily, and creating a low cost mechanism for plantation development, the MTS was introduced as a

collaborative forest restoration method to help provide livelihood opportunities for forest communities, while also addressing the country's forest loss and timber shortage . The system allows for communities to enter reserves and

conduct farming practices on reserve in return for providing labour to plant trees and subsequently maintain them.

<!-- image -->

## 2.2 Component-by-component analysis

## Component 1: Off-reserve, degraded savannah forests restored under self-financing community management in CREMA (total cost: USD 7.76 million; GCF cost: USD 5.78 million, or 74.5 per cent)

8. This component aims to establish an effective forest and land-use management system and increase the adaptive capacities of the local communities. It will place 200,000 hectares of degraded forest and grassland under sustainable forest management, reducing carbon emissions by 1,271,899 tCO2eq for the project duration of seven years; and there will be an additional 220,000 hectares where local communities will engage in improved fire management, thereby reducing carbon emissions by an additional 905,952 tCO2eq for the project duration.
9. The project interventions under this component will support strengthening and developing CREMA as the management groups responsible for establishing sustainable charcoal and wood fuel production systems within the forest areas.

## Component 2: Degraded shea parklands restored through public-private partnerships (total cost: USD 19.36 million; GCF cost: USD 10.40 million, or 53.7 per cent)

10. This component focuses on supporting the restoration of 100,000 hectares of degraded shea parkland areas through increased planting of shea and other trees. A total of 1.75 million shea trees will be restocked in the parklands with higher and quicker-yielding varieties of shea, and 400,000 other high-value trees will be planted within the agroforestry system. By ensuring the future supply of shea through performance-based contracts between members of the Global Shea Alliance (GSA) and women's groups, this component aims to create a long-term partnership between the communities and the buyers. 2  Increasing the economic value of shea trees is expected to change the incentive system to tree protection.
11. Other interventions under this component will include increasing awareness of tree planting opportunities, establishing shea nurseries, improving extension services to support shea tree management, and farmer-managed regeneration by planting new trees within bush fallow systems. Particular focus will be on supporting the establishment of women's collectives to function as processing hubs, which is expected to increase the adaptive capacity of 20,000 women through improvements in the shea butter value chain (e.g. more efficient collection of shea, access to new technologies, aggregation in warehouses and direct sales to processors for higher prices).
12. The proposed interventions are expected to reduce carbon emissions by 58,520 tCO2eq from shea planting and 26,663 tCO2eq from other trees, with a further 165,135 tCO2eq from reductions in wood fuel use over the project duration of seven years.

## Component 3: Modified taungya system plantations and fire management in forest reserves (total cost: USD 23 million; GCF cost: USD 10.67 million, or 46.4 per cent)

13. The NSZ contains 66 forest reserves covering an area of 638,800 hectares as well as two protected areas covering 512,200 hectares, of which 455,700 hectares are in Mole National Park, one of the country's flagship conservation and tourism areas. Most of the forest reserves are degraded and deforested and, by applying MTS, this component aims to provide a mechanism for cost-effective plantation development while increasing community ownership of the forest reserves, which will create incentives to help reduce the incidence of fire as well as other forms of encroachment in the reserves.
14. Developing effective private sector based commercial plantation development in the NSZ has been challenging due to limited access to finance and lack of incentives to protect the forest reserves. Under this component, the interventions will support a programme of MTS

<!-- image -->

- plantations in 12 forest reserves. It will include benefit sharing that incentivizes communities to participate in the planting of mixed stands of species, including local hardwood species.
15. For the project duration of seven years, it is expected that carbon emissions will be reduced by 1,012,958 tCO2eq from MTS plantations and 342,471 tCO2eq from improved regeneration of natural forest.
- Component 4: Integrated monitoring system at the project level implemented, and REDD-plus systems strengthened (total cost: USD 2.02 million; GCF cost: USD 2 million, or 99.3 per cent)
16. Woodland and land management in the NSZ has only recently begun to receive attention and investment, when Ghana adopted a nested approach for implementing its REDD-plus strategy in a phased manner. The initial focus was on the high forest zone but the country is scaling up to cover the other distinct major ecological zones of the country, notably the NSZ.
17. The component has two objectives:
- (a) Enabling the completion of Ghana's Warsaw Framework architecture; and
- (b) Monitoring implementation and outcomes of the project to ensure that safeguards are applied and respected.
18. The interventions under this component will support the operationalization and strengthening of the national forest monitoring system, and inform the stepwise improvement of the forest reference level and the safeguards information system for reporting on how Ghana is addressing and respecting safeguards. The data and information gathered from the NSZ through this component will be integrated with the National Climate Data Hub within the Environmental Protection Agency to facilitate effective reporting to the United Nations Framework Convention on Climate Change.

## Project management (total cost: USD 2.4 million; GCF cost: USD 1.24 million, or 51.9 per cent)

19. GCF grant financing represents 51.9 per cent of the total project management costs. The requested project management costs amount to 4.1 per cent of the total grant requested from GCF.

## III. Assessment of performance against investment criteria

## 3.1 Impact potential Scale: High

20. The project has a significant impact potential as a cross-cutting project. Through the improved management of shea parklands and savannah forests, the project aims to help reduce emissions from deforestation and forest degradation. These contributions are significant, amounting to a reduction of 25,248,972 tCO2eq over 20 years and 6,139,433 tCO2eq for the 7 years' duration of the project. These significant results will mainly be achieved by strengthening the CREMA and their key role in restoring and conserving shea parklands, improved fuelwood production and improved fire management.
21. The project will contribute to the restoration of degraded savannah and shea parklands. A significant impact is that, once restored, these lands will carry on acting as safety nets and main livelihood strategies for the very vulnerable and poor communities residing in the project area.
22. The project will directly strengthen the livelihoods and climate resilience of vulnerable communities and strongly support them in gaining strengthened capacities of forest management, fuelwood production, fire management and access to finance through the CREMA structure.

<!-- image -->

GCF/B.26/02/Add.09

Page 97

## 3.2 Paradigm shift potential Scale: High

23. The project will help to restore and conserve the landscapes that sustain millions of rural poor in the NSZ in Ghana and will pilot a very innovative approach for conserving the shea parklands and securing the future production of shea nuts. The piloted supported across the components will enable Ghana to scale up the interventions in other parts of the country.
24. The local communities are at the centre of the project and will be extensively engaged and involved in the restoration and conservation efforts of the degraded shea parklands and savannah landscapes in general. Through the CREMA support, communities will build key capacities and improve coordination and organization of the management of landscapes and access to finance.
25. The project is a very innovative basis on which to secure the future of the shea landscapes. Furthermore, the involvement of the GSA will help build experience that can later be scaled up and used in the development of a regional shea landscape programme.

## 3.3 Sustainable development potential Scale: High

26. The activities developed by the project, with the involvement of the local communities, will directly contribute to protecting and conserving the environment that sustains the livelihoods of the most vulnerable and poor in the target area. The project will help conserve important biodiversity and secure flow of ecosystem services to the rural poor.
27. The nature of the activities will enable the communities to diversify their livelihoods and gain key capacities and organizational skills to foster a larger transformation of the shea parklands. This will also have very important socioeconomic impacts beyond the project area.

## 3.4 Needs of the recipient Scale: Medium-high

28. The project targets one of the poorest and most climate vulnerable areas in Ghana, the NSZ, where shea parklands exist. The project will help to improve the livelihoods of the people in the project area, increase community ownership of the management of important ecosystems such as the shea parklands and support communities in improving their forest management skills and gaining access to finance.
29. Furthermore, the project will help to create long-term transformation of community organizations, through its support of the CREMA, which will be able to adapt to new challenges and demands in the future due to the experience gained this project.

## 3.5 Country ownership Scale: High

30. Country ownership is considered high. The Government prioritizes development of the NSZ and its important ecosystems. The project is fully aligned with the country's nationally determined contribution and numerous strategies in the forest and ecosystem sectors, and is part of the long-term goals the Government has for the northern zone and the shea parklands. There is a very strong commitment from national institutions and, because the Government with the Forestry Commission comprise the executing entity (EE), full ownership is with the Government.

<!-- image -->

GCF/B.26/02/Add.09

Page 98

## 3.6 Efficiency and effectiveness Scale: High

31. The project is financed through grants and in-kind contributions, but the nature of the project opens up opportunities to attract significant private sector finance. The experiences and structures built within the project can help to foster an enabling environment for the private sector. Shea butter is an important ingredient in many food and cosmetic products and, through the partnership of the GSA, there are many untapped possibilities to strengthen partnerships and leverage additional private sector finance.

## IV. Assessment of consistency with GCF safeguards and policies

## 4.1 Environmental and social safeguards

32. The accredited entity (AE) has screened the project using its social and environmental screening procedure and has assessed the project to be of moderate risk. The Secretariat confirms that this is equivalent to Category B in accordance with the Environmental and Social Policy categorization and is within the AE's accreditation level. The AE has submitted a social and environmental screening form and prepared an environmental and social management framework (ESMF), which have been disclosed on the websites of both the AE and GCF. The ESMF provides for the development of specific management plans and disclosure with stakeholders during project implementation.
33. The ESMF identifies key potential project risks and impacts, and outlines possible management measures, including managing the impacts of using agricultural inputs such as fertilizers, managing other solid waste generated in operating nurseries, and implementing shea restoration activities. Creation of the tree plantations is expected to be undertaken on identified lands which have been certified through the Forestry Commission and are government-owned forest reserve land.
34. Through the social and environmental screening of the AE, the indigenous peoples standard has been triggered. To mitigate potential risks, including reduced access and use of resources, the ESMF provides guidance on developing a livelihood action plan. Proposed interventions will also be decided with local stakeholders, and changes in land use and management will have to be voluntarily agreed upon. The establishment of the CREMA will be driven by the communities, who hold the CREMA lands communally. Agreements between traditional authorities and the minority or transient communities (e.g. Fulani herders) to enable certain uses of land, such as for grazing purposes, will also have to be established. The environmental and social impact assessment will identify the presence of indigenous peoples for each of the specific sites and if there is a potential impact UNDP will, in line with the GCF Indigenous Peoples Policy, prepare an indigenous peoples plan. If needed, this will include a plan for culturally appropriate consultations and procedures for obtaining free, prior and informed consent.
35. Although there are several protected areas within the scope of the project's targeted sites, no activities are expected to be undertaken in these areas. Rather, restoration activities in the community-managed areas and in agricultural lands in the surrounding forest reserves are envisioned to further disincentivize activities such as harvesting for fuelwood in these areas. There is also a plan to plant appropriate site-adjusted species. Local labour will be employed, and such employment will be governed by national laws and standards relating to employment and workers' health and safety. The ESMF also provides guidance for avoiding culturally sensitive areas that may be affected by the project.
36. The project describes the institutional arrangements involved in implementing the ESMF and the corresponding management of environmental and social issues. A proposed

<!-- image -->

GCF/B.26/02/Add.09

budget to conduct further assessments and development of management plans, including providing capacity-building training to project staff, is also incorporated in the project. During development, the project engaged in extensive consultations with various stakeholders in the development of the project, including private companies and civil society organizations, and such consultations are expected to continue throughout project implementation. The AE has an established grievance redress mechanism, which describes eligibility requirements and processes for affected parties to lodge grievances and resolve their concerns. At the EE and project level, the ESMF provides structure and operational guidelines in providing feedback to affected persons and redress of grievances. A feedback and grievance redress mechanism desk will be established, to be staffed by a specialist. Traditionally recognized institutions also provide avenues for dispute resolution within their respective areas. In line with the Indigenous Peoples Policy of the GCF, the GCF Independent Redress Mechanism and the Secretariat's indigenous peoples focal point will also be available for affected local communities.

## 4.2 Gender policy

37. The AE has provided a gender assessment and gender action plan, and therefore complies with the requirements of the Gender Policy of the GCF.
38. The AE's gender assessment indicates the country's commitment to gender equality and women's empowerment. Ghana has signed international conventions such as the Convention on the Elimination of All Forms of Discrimination Against Women. In addition, Ghana's constitution prohibits discrimination on the basis of gender, and the country has a National Gender Policy (2015) which is aimed at mainstreaming gender equality and women's empowerment concerns into the national development process. Further, Ghana has frameworks and plans, such as the national medium-term development plans, and the affirmative action plan with a 40 per cent quota for women on various platforms.
39. Although the enabling environment exists, implementation of the various policies, plans and frameworks is challenged. Cultural practices and patriarchal values, and existing customary and statutory laws on marriage, divorce, inheritance, custody and land tenure have significant implications on access, use and control of resources by women and men. Existing barriers to achieving gender equality and women's empowerment including the feminization of poverty, socio-cultural and traditional beliefs, inadequate basic social services, low representation of women in politics, and violence against women. Further, women in general earn less than men, are less educated, and are excluded from access to and ownership of land. The lack of ownership of land results in the inability of women to access loans from formal channels. Women lack awareness of land laws and of the guarantees these laws can provide them. Female headed households make up about 30 per cent of rural households but face additional challenges. Data and information provided in the funding proposal indicate that women in Ghana face issues in accessing resources and services, training and participating in decision-making.
40. In rural Ghana, shea butter extraction is a common activity among women (90 per cent of income source for women), and it is a female-dominated production process. Women have the knowledge and experience in processing shea nuts and producing the shea butter. Nevertheless, women face persistent gender inequalities in the shea production industry, which limit their ability to fully benefit from production, and they face abuse and unfair labour practices. The production that women engage in is labour intensive and time consuming, and is a manual labour process for which they earn about USD 2 after five days of labour. There are seasonal fluctuations that affect women's ability to supply shea consistently, and this often affects prices. Women's ability to buy and stockpile nuts, which would have otherwise supported the women to stabilize the prices, is constrained because they operate individually and at small scale. Women process shea nuts through arduous tasks and are also faced with challenges in accessing improved technologies to increase efficiency and to produce their

<!-- image -->

Page 100

- product in quality. Shea trees are located far from their homes, so women will have to travel long distance, facing dangers on the way, and work in competitive environments when collecting the shea nuts. Women have limited information on market prices and their immediate need for cash puts them at a disadvantage; thereby forcing them to sell their produce at whatever price is on offer. Moreover, women operate individually at a small scale and are not leveraging the benefits of collective action which could improve their efficiency and bargaining power, and could help to regulate prices.
41. The gender action plan describes activities that will address the challenges faced by women shea producers, having set a baseline, indicators, targets, timelines and budgets. The AE will put in place a gender expert to support the implementation of the gender action plan, and the expert will work with the REDD-plus gender sub-working group to ensure the implementation of the gender action plan. The gender action plan includes various activities that are linked to the findings of the assessment but also indicates the need to conduct further assessment to ensure that the action plan responds to the actual needs of women and the community, and to ensure that further issues are addressed (e.g. the need for additional gendersensitive and sex-disaggregated baseline data, including on capacity gaps, time use/saving, gendered roles and responsibilities in communities, violence against women). The activities already included in the action plan are aimed at building women's capacity (e.g. in audit, business, contract and warehouse management and direct marketing), build women's skills and capacity to produce collectively (e.g. cooperative set up and management ) while also providing technologies (fuel- and water-efficient processing technologies and quality control) to increase efficiency and increase the incomes (by 30-50 per cent) of women engaged in shea production while also investing in increased participation of women in various processes and activities to ensure their enhanced participation and capacity in decision-making.
42. The AE is recommended to look closely at the potential issue of men co-opting the activities of women as the production process becomes more lucrative. It might be worth investing in exploring whether investment in sensitization of women and men about gender issues could assist, in addition to engaging men on other components of the programme. Further, while the environmental and social impact assessment is being conducted it is also recommended that challenges to women's access to the project grievance mechanism be explored, in addition to the violence against women. It is also recommended that the response provided to the question on co-opting in the gender assessment be included.

## 4.3 Risks

## 4.3.1. Overall programme assessment (medium risk)

43. The GCF is requested to provide a grant of USD 30.1 million to enhance forest carbon stocks by restoring savannah forests and shea parklands and implementing a monitoring system in Ghana. The Forestry Commission (the EE) and multiple private sector companies are providing co-financing of USD 15 million and USD 9 million, respectively, by way of grants and in-kind contributions.
44. It is expected that the project will lead to revenue generation from the sale of timber wood, non-timber forestry products, shea trees and MTS plantation. Despite the revenuegenerating potential, the AE stated that the GCF grant is needed because of the public nature of the project and the current state of Ghana's public finances which limit the Government's ability to take on even concessional debt.

<!-- image -->

- 4.3.2. Accredited entity/executing entity capability to execute the current programme (medium risk)
45. UNDP is the AE for this project. The AE has had representation in the country since 1978 and has been playing a leading role in supporting the mainstreaming of climate change and environmental considerations into development planning in the country.
46. The Forestry Commission is the EE of the project. It has decentralized offices at the regional and district level. Over the past ten years, the EE has experience in implementing projects relating to forestry and land use, with a budget ranging between USD 1.5 million and USD 8.2 million. The AE carried out the capacity assessment of the EE and the overall risk rating of the EE is low.

## 4.3.3. Programme-specific execution risks (medium risk)

47. Governance for benefit sharing and reinvestment: The project is expected to operationalize the CREMA from the revenues from the forest, shea parklands and MTS plantation. A community or revolving fund will be set up in each CREMA. The project will set up the process towards a full devolution of rights on financial management and relevant investment decisions (e.g. sharing of benefits and costs including operation and maintenance, eligibility criteria for the investments) and this process will be determined by the Community Resource Management Committee, with oversight and guidance from the EE, an advisory group and the project management unit. The funding proposal states that there will be multiple signatories on a local bank account, and frequent oversight and auditing of accounts by the CREMA Executive Committee and a third party will be in place. It is critical that the AE/EE carry out regular oversight during the project implementation and that the EE appropriately manages these governance structures beyond the project implementation. The proposed revolving mechanism is largely focused at the community level. Since the project expects the revenue to be generated in the medium to long term (especially for Outputs 2 and 3), the project may benefit if the AE/EE consider strengthening the long-term oversight plans at the national level.
48. Private sector involvement: Output 2 will be implemented via a public-private partnership. Activities to be financed by GCF and the private sector are separated, and the results of activities funded by both parties will be included in the performance-based payment contract. This output is expected to create a long-term relationship between buyers and communities and to engage the private sector in the early development of supply chain. A total of USD 9 million of co-financing from the private sector is indicated as per their co-financing letters; however, the letters are not legally binding. The AE clarified that GSA will ensure that complementary financing will be sourced and this will be confirmed prior to the FAA execution.
49. Carbon credit sale: A private sector co-financier may wish to sell carbon credits and proceeds can be used for co-financing. If that is the case, the mitigation impact (e.g. emission reductions) will need to be adjusted accordingly. While the AE clarified that the project does not have any plans to sell the carbon credits that will be generated from the project and that the carbon credits will be counted by the country towards its nationally determined contribution, any sales of carbon credits should be properly monitored to account for effective emission reductions. The project will reduce leakage in adjoining areas outside of the project site. The integrated monitoring system and strengthened REDD-plus system should be able to differentiate the emission reductions from different areas to avoid any potential double counting in the future.
50. Project viability and concessionality: A financial analysis has been carried out for each output. While Output 2 results in negative net present value, Output 1 and Output 3 (amounting to 56 per cent of the total project cost) result in the financial internal rates of return of 19 per cent and 48 per cent, respectively. The AE responded that, despite the financial viability,

<!-- image -->

- conventional private investors would not take up this investment because the proposed structures are low cost in nature which would not have been possible for the private sector to maintain. Also, the benefits need to be accrued to communities while social and environment safeguards are being respected. Therefore, the GCF grant funding is still necessary for the initial investment and to engage the private sector. The economic analysis results in the economic internal rate of return of 45 per cent with the social cost of carbon being USD 30/tCO2eq.

## 4.3.4. Compliance risk (medium risk)

51. A review of the proposed activities does not indicate any unusually high risk for money laundering, terrorist financing or prohibited practices, although the usual risk for such illicit activities does remain.  The AE has described its approach to assessing these risks, monitoring them, and taking measures to mitigate such risks. The AE has described its internal controls approach, as set forth in its accreditation. Based on the information provided, the compliance risk is assessed as Medium, subject to revision should additional information or circumstances warrant.

## 4.3.5. GCF portfolio concentration risk (low risk)

52. In case of approval, the impact of this proposal on the GCF portfolio risk remains nonmaterial and within the risk appetite in terms of concentration level, results area or single proposal.

## 4.3.6. Recommendation

53. It is recommended that the Board consider the above factors in its decision.

## 4.4 Fiduciary

54. UNDP, in its capacity as the AE, will represent the interests of the parties concerned that provide funding and/or technical expertise to the project.
55. The Forestry Commission of Ghana (Climate Change Directorate), as the EE, assumes full responsibility and accountability for the effective use of UNDP resources. It is accountable to UNDP for managing the project to deliver the planned outputs and manage risk in accordance with the agreed project document; reporting fairly and accurately on project progress and risk against agreed workplans and results frameworks, in accordance with the reporting schedule and formats included in the project agreement; and maintaining documentation and evidence

<!-- image -->

- that describes the proper and prudent use of project resources in conformity with the project agreement, and applicable regulations and procedures.
56. The relationship between UNDP and the EE will be regulated by the project document (or subsidiary agreement) signed by both parties. The GSA will act as responsible party in the delivery of the project outputs by carrying out (or leading the implementation of) activities 2.1 and 2.2. The GSA will be directly accountable to the Forestry Commission in accordance with the terms of their agreement or contract.
57. The relationship between the GCF and UNDP as AE will be regulated by the accreditation master agreement (AMA) and the FAA.
58. The financial management and procurement of this project will be guided by UNDP financial rules and regulations. The project will be implemented following the national implementation modality (NIM) of the UNDP, according to the Standard Basic Assistance Agreement between UNDP and the Government of Ghana, and the policies and procedures outlined in the UNDP Programme and Operations Policies and Procedures.
59. The project will be audited in accordance with UNDP policies and procedures on audits, informed by and together with any specific requirements agreed in the AMA. According to the current audit policies, UNDP will be appointing the auditors. UNDP advances cash funds on a quarterly basis to the EE for the implementation of agreed and approved programme activities, in accordance with UNDP standard policies and the NIM Guidelines. The EE reports back expenditure via a financial report on quarterly basis to UNDP.

## 4.5 Results monitoring and reporting

60. The Secretariat notes that the project proponent has provided a theory of change that articulates the project's ultimate objective, outcomes and impacts as well as how a paradigm shift is advanced. Key barriers have been articulated with respect to the eventual outcomes of the project, namely that:
2. (a) Communities will be able to maintain socially beneficial and financially viable management of lands and natural resources over the long term; and
3. (b) Deforestation and forest degradation will be reduced and adaptive capacity increased.
61. Overall, the Secretariat considers that the set of interventions proposed address the barriers and the proponent's causal logic can be interred from the theory of change diagram.
62. However, the theory of change diagram does not articulate the risks that might have an impact on achieving the expected results and does not clearly state any assumptions. It is notable that the part of the intervention which focuses on the involvement of the private sector in public-private partnerships was not mentioned as a particular risk in the theory of change diagram.
63. Furthermore, the theory of change does not mention a very critical element: behavioural changes. This factor should be articulated as an important element of driving the expected/predicted changes, especially because the expected long-term results are that sustainable, replicable and gender-responsive community-based forest management will be one of the eventual outcomes of the proposed set of interventions.
64. Finally, the project's outputs are based on the premise that by removing the technical, governance, institutional and financial barriers to increasing the value of trees, non-timber forest products and ecosystem services in the landscapes in the NSZ, communities will be able to maintain socially beneficial and financially viable management of their lands and natural resources over the long term. However, although this is somewhat reflected at the outcome level in the theory of change (i.e. under the forest development master plan), the elements

<!-- image -->

- (activities, outputs) are not clearly articulated. This has a downstream impact in the sense that there is no project-level indicator/result that can be used to measure the changes with respect to the institutional or financial barriers being removed.
65. The logical framework has been designed using relevant details such as reporting on core indicators for mitigation and adaptation, as this is a cross-cutting project. The relevant impact and outcome indicators for the result areas and the project targets as per the GCF results management framework/performance management framework have been selected.
66. However, at the project/programme output level (i.e. project performance indicators), especially for output 2 (degraded shea parklands restored through public-private partnerships) there is an overreliance on quantitative indictors which do not adequately capture the expected change in the skill level for women being trained, especially with respect to business skills. Essentially, the questions of uptake of the training by the women and their improved capacity as a result of the training are not directly addressed in the logical framework. This means that the actual change and/or improvement in their capacity will not be measured.
67. Additionally, Barrier 4, which addresses the issue that prior interventions to share awareness and abate the causes of the declining shea tree stock have not been conducive to investments for the private sector, has not been reflected at the output level in the logical framework using indicators that will also measure this change. This implies that, for project monitoring and reporting, there will be no data to understand progress or lack of progress regarding Barrier 4 or the activities outlined in the logical framework that are intended to support the actions to address those barriers.
68. Furthermore, at the activity level, particularly Activity 2.3 which is to 'Enhance the enabling environment for climate-smart shea and upscaling of finance and investments' there is no mention of how this will be measured and monitored.
69. The funding proposal implementation timetable has been completed appropriately. It shows all activities and key milestones associated with each phase of the project and they are consistent with the logical framework.
70. The arrangements for monitoring, evaluation and reporting as set out in section E.7 of the funding proposal are adequate.

## 4.6 Legal assessment

71. The AMA was signed with the AE on 5 August 2016, and it became effective on 23 November 2016.
72. The AE has provided a legal opinion/certificate confirming that it has obtained all internal approvals and it has the capacity and authority to implement the project.
73. The proposed project will be implemented in Ghana, a country in which the GCF is not provided with privileges and immunities. This means that, among other things, the GCF is not protected against litigation or expropriation in this country, which risks need to be further assessed. A draft privileges and immunities agreement was sent to the Government of Ghana on 19 November 2015 and the agreement remains under the Government's review. There are no ongoing negotiations.
74. The Heads of the Independent Redress Mechanism and Independent Integrity Unit have both expressed that it would not be legally feasible to undertake their redress activities and/or investigations, as appropriate, in countries where the GCF is not provided with relevant privileges and immunities. Therefore, it is recommended that disbursements by the GCF are made only after the GCF has obtained satisfactory protection against litigation and expropriation in the country, or has been provided with appropriate privileges and immunities.

<!-- image -->

## 4.7 List of proposed conditions (including legal)

75. In order to mitigate risk, it is recommended that any approval by the Board is made subject to the following conditions: (a) Signature of the funded activity agreement in a form and substance satisfactory to the Secretariat within 180 days from the date of Board approval or the date when all internal approvals by the Accredited Entity are obtained; and
2. (b) Completion of legal due diligence to the satisfaction of the Secretariat.

## Independent Technical Advisory Panel's assessment of FP137

| Proposal name:          | Ghana Shea Landscape Emission Reductions Project   |
|-------------------------|----------------------------------------------------|
| Accredited entity:      | United Nations Development Programme (UNDP)        |
| Country/(ies):          | Ghana                                              |
