## I. Assessment of the independent Technical Advisory Panel

## 1.1 Impact potential Scale: Medium to high

1. Ghana, with a population of over 29 million people and a surface area of 238,535 km² has very high rates of deforestation and forest degradation. Specifically, the northern savannah zone (NSZ), where the proposed project is expected to take place, is a fragile ecosystem sensitive to the effects of climate change with an area of 9.7 million hectares (ha) and a population of over 5 million people with high poverty rates. The region lost 77 per cent of its forest cover between 2001 and 2015, causing the removal of not only environmental services but also economic products (e.g. shea kernels and charcoal) that have acted as both a cornerstone of the economy and a critical safety net for the poor. The region has nearly 400 million shea and other trees growing naturally in traditional farming systems.
2. The conversion to agriculture, wood fuel harvesting and charcoal production has been the main target of deforestation. The loss of forest cover results in a more abundant growth of grasses, which, in turn, results in more intense fires in the dry season, frequently causing further forest degradation and deforestation and preventing the regeneration of forest areas already impacted by other drivers. It is estimated that 1,058,492 ha has been deforested, resulting in emissions of 3,568,400 tonnes of carbon dioxide equivalent (tCO2eq) per year from NSZ.
3. The proposed project will contribute to enhancing forest carbon stocks across the landscape by 1) restoring 200,000 ha of off-reserve savannah forests/woodlands and place them under self-financing community management in community resource management areas (CREMAs); 2) restoring 100,000 ha of degraded shea parklands through public-private partnerships; 3) creating 25,500 ha of modified taungya system (MTS)/forest plantation in severely degraded forest reserves; and 4) implementing an integrated monitoring system and completing the national REDD-plus architecture for safeguards, forest monitoring and reporting systems.
4. The GCF funds and co-finance will support women collectors in restocking 1.75 million shea trees, which can live for hundreds of years and bear abundant crops during much of their lifetime. In addition, 400,000 other high-value trees within CREMA and agroforestry systems will be planted and nurtured, providing a wide range of ecosystem services, timber and nontimber products.
5. These actions will deliver 6.139 MtCO2eq in emission reductions and removals over the first seven years of the project's lifetime and 25.24 MtCO2eq over 20 years. The impacts of the proposed project on emission reductions from the forest sector across NSZ have been estimated based on information and data presented primarily from Ghana's forest reference level

<!-- image -->

- submitted to the United Nations Framework Convention on Climate Change (UNFCCC) secretariat in January 2017 and subsequently revised in June 2017.
6. However, there is a high risk that these estimates could be overestimated. The NSZ area has been facing several challenges for years, as stated above, including fires, droughts, overgrazing (involving nomad pastoral Fulani herdsmen) and the limited availability of water. This risk will increase as climate change progresses.
7. The independent Technical Advisory Panel (TAP) asked the proponents for an estimate of the water balance to sustain the shea plantations, to which the response was that only seedlings in the nurseries would be watered for two years and that sufficient water can be obtained through leveraging the One Village One Dam initiative and the dugouts and aquifers that exist in the landscape. Transplanted seedlings and farmer-managed natural regeneration will be rain-fed. In terms of water balance under future climate change scenarios the proponents have indicated that several studies show that there will be sufficient groundwater to recharge water bodies and enough water to continue irrigation of crops and trees. Since water scenarios will play a crucial role in ensuring success or failure in terms of tree survival, together with the above-mentioned aspects, it is crucial to have adequate water balance estimates.
8. In terms of tree survival, the proponents state that their projection includes a 50 per cent mortality rate for shea seedlings with a buffer of 250,000 trees on top of this. (the amount targeted for planting and budgeted for was 3.5 million trees plus additions from natural regeneration of 250,000). They have also made provision for a mortality of 20 per cent of the non-shea trees.
9. In terms of forest fires the proponents explain that the objective is to replace destructive mid- to late-season forest and fallow fires with early fires in the dry season that have minimum negative impact on forest regeneration and will help to reduce the effects of climate change currently being experienced in NSZ. The fire management strategy will promote early burning in the first month of the dry season, once fire management capacities in the form of fire brigades are in place.
10. A REDD-plus investment plan is currently being developed at the country level. The project proposal expects to contribute to Ghana's national approach to REDD-plus through the implementation of actions towards safeguarding, monitoring and reporting requirements and enabling the attainment of Ghana's global mitigation role through its nationally determined contribution. However, this is just one small component of the overall project, aiming basically to integrate data from NSZ into the national forest monitoring system (NFMS), update the forest reference level and land cover maps to develop a clear assessment of past and current trends in forest cover change within NSZ and procure services to develop the NFMS web portal to host data, maps and other information which can be accessed by key stakeholders.
11. Ghana is assessing its eligibility to receive results-based payments from the NSZ and other programmes by tapping into opportunities presented by the operationalization of cooperative and voluntary approaches and the mechanism established through Article 6 of the Paris Agreement. In relation to this possibility, the proposal does not include any concrete actions or estimates, as the REDD-plus architecture is under development.
12. The project presents adaptation benefits, as actions will be supporting poverty alleviation and rural livelihoods through increased incomes and access to natural resources, positively affecting 100,200 direct beneficiaries (78,850 women and 21,350 men) and 540,200 indirect beneficiaries. The project has the potential to improve social and environmental resilience, but as explained in sections 1.2 and 1.6 below on the paradigm shift and effectiveness and efficiency, respectively, the sustainability and management of the immense tree landscape need to be better estimated to ensure medium- and long-term adaptation benefits to communities.

<!-- image -->

GCF/B.26/02/Add.09

Page 108

13. Overall, the combined mitigation outputs to restore savannah forests, shea parklands and MTS forest plantations, together with the adaptation impacts, have a medium to high potential impact. It should be noted that during the initial years of the project, communities will continue to live from charcoal and wood fuel in a business as usual model until the shea trees and other productive trees grow to ensure their livelihoods. Since there is a potential for the remaining forests to deteriorate further during this period, the project will need to look for viable sustainable options for the initial years and pay attention to the overall sustainability of the project interventions.

## 1.2 Paradigm shift potential Scale: Medium to high

## 1.2.1. Innovation

14. The project's first component focuses on supporting 10 CREMAs, groups of communities that have agreed to work together for the sustainable use of shared natural resources under locally developed consensual constitutions, which are backed by the relevant district by-laws and management plans, leading to official recognition. Their main activities will include the production and processing of wood fuels and improving silvicultural methods with a view to limiting the harvesting of forest products and timber outside CREMA and forest reserves. The sustainability of the CREMAs is grounded in their capacity to produce wood fuels, non-timber forest products and timber that will provide livelihood diversification opportunities to communities. CREMAs will also be responsible for fire management with the support of the Forest Services Division.
15. The project proposal explains that CREMA development is not a rapid or easy process and that besides community consensus-building there is a need to strengthen the communities' economic base with an equitable sharing scheme. The project is therefore proposing the creation of community CREMA funds. It is proposed that each community fund in each CREMA be rigorously set up, with accountable, gender-responsive and transparent governance systems that can be replicated in other CREMAs and that the CREMA Executive Committee would manage the funds. A small percentage (up to 7 per cent) of the revenue from the revenuegenerating activities that the CREMAs will be undertaking will be used to capitalize the funds. Municipal and district assemblies are also expected to contribute to the funds. However, since GCF resources will not be used to contribute to the community funds, there could be a potential risk of their not being sustained, as has been the experience in the past.
16. The second project component will support women collectors in restocking 1.75 million shea trees in the parklands. Shea trees have been established in the NSZ region for many years and are able to fruit for hundreds of years. The project will support higher and quicker yielding varieties of shea to ensure future supply to enable commercial contracts between members of the Global Shea Alliance (GSA) and women's cooperatives. 1  Another 400,000 high-value trees will be planted within agroforestry systems. Linkages between shea cooperatives that use wood fuel and wood fuel operators will strengthen local demand for sustainable wood fuel.
17. The third component will focus on restoring forest woodlands and creating taungya forest plantations. NSZ contains 66 forest reserves covering an area of 638,800 ha as well as two protected areas covering 512,200 ha, of which 455,700 ha are in Mole National Park, most of them very degraded. For output 3 the Forestry Commission will establish MTS plantations on deforested portions of 12 forest reserves that have been identified by the Forestry Commission and fire management in both savannah forests and grasslands in the same reserves. The Forestry Commission will invest in restoring a further 7,000 ha with direct contracts with the

1  3.5 million trees will be planted in the parklands and in CREMA,s and it is expected that, at a minimum, half of these will survive at the end of seven years.

<!-- image -->

- communities, manage the plantations and set in place and oversee the benefit-sharing arrangements.
18. The proposal spends too much resources in capacity-building and very few in sustaining the management of the landscape and the governance of CREMAs and shea cooperatives run by communities. The proposal assumes that the revenues from timber, non-timber forest products, charcoal and shea kernels will provide the start-up capital to set up the CREMA funds and over time to fund activities such as fire management, management of areas for fuel wood, tree planting or any other green activity to maintain the forest standing. After three sets of questions and a direct call from the independent TAP, the proponents have shifted the budgets so as to allocate a further USD 1 million to directly support the activities of the communities. However, as explained in section 1.6 below on efficiency and effectiveness, the independent TAP believes this is not sufficient and proposes a revised economic analysis that includes revenues and costs from productive landscapes as a condition.
19. Moreover, the expected revenues from plantations will not materialize until the shea forests and other valuable forests are mature enough to provide non-timber forest products. During the initial phases, the communities will need to work under their own expenses aside from some inputs provided by the project in the form of seeds and materials for nurseries and plantations and various expenses related to training, venues, snacks and travel.
20. The private sector is providing in kind co-financing in the form of the construction/refurbishment of warehouses and seeds (only 10 per cent of the expected cofinancing), and the provision of training in different aspects of the shea business to communities. The increase in quality of the shea kernels will eventually be to their advantage in sustaining their profitable businesses. The partnerships with non-governmental organizations (NGOs) concern service contracts that will cost more than USD 3 million to train 70 farming communities in setting up nurseries and planting shea trees.
21. The project expects to create a long-term relationship between buyers and communities, including the large companies in the cosmetics and food industries that buy shea-based ingredients through ingredient manufacturers. To this end, companies could invest in supporting the communities in maintaining the vast plantations and could also increase the prices of the shea kernels, ensuring an equitable distribution of benefits.
22. There is a paradigm shift in demonstrating that restoration (tree planting) can be achieved at scale for shea, especially in the large savannah landscape currently prone to desertification and land degradation. However, the sustainability of this investment could be at risk if the project does not plan a valid mechanism to ensure that communities are able to maintain the landscapes after project completion.
23. The project provides limited support to the REDD-plus architecture. As explained above, only limited resources are devoted to supporting its development and it might take time until the REDD-plus scheme is effectively implemented to allow the proposed project to receive payments for eligible emission reductions.
24. The theory of change is well presented with a long-term vision of change to reduce emissions from deforestation and forest degradation through sustainable forest management and enhanced carbon stocks, improved resilience of ecosystems and ecosystem services and improved resilience and enhanced livelihoods of the vulnerable population.

## 1.2.2. Potential for knowledge and learning and enabling environment

25. The project has a high potential to create an enabling environment for knowledge generation. It will include applied research and promote social cohesion with collective benefits and capacity-building activities with communities under the CREMA model.

<!-- image -->

26. The project will focus on capacity-building to support the aggregation of shea kernels and direct marketing through performance-based payment contracts for NGOs and private sector companies to deliver services and engage with women's cooperatives and farmers. However, the large investments given to NGOs to provide training to communities could be more effectively delivered through training of trainers, bearing in mind that the communities have experience of living in forests and know the shea business and could well be their own best trainers.
27. The project will build on the experience of creating the CREMA model, which was designed to protect wildlife resources, to support the government in developing policy reforms to enable the model to be used in forest and tree tenure and benefit-sharing. It will be interesting to see how the project ensures the rights associated with community 'rights to trees', which also relates to land tenure.
28. In terms of community forest plantations and their benefits under MTS, the taungya farmers would be organized into smaller groups to ensure the effective training and sensitization of farmers. Periodic technical training for farmers is intended to be provided by both the Forestry Commission and the Ministry of Agriculture. Through a proposed learning platform 13,000 farmers will be able to share knowledge and experience to effectively manage their forests.

## 1.2.3. Contribution to the regulatory framework and policies

29. The project is expected to develop a baseline that will serve to support NFMS to measure the effectiveness of and the benefits from REDD-plus interventions. Even though the forest reference level and NFMS are essential tools for demonstrating the transparency and accountability credentials of Ghana's REDD-plus work and for seeking results-based payments under UNFCCC, it seems that the country needs to do a lot more work to ensure an operational REDD-plus architecture.

## 1.2.4. Scalability and replicability

30. The project will support the structuring of the CREMAs with a participative governance arrangement that will allow the CREMAs to define land-use and investment planning, develop a strategy and set of activities (management plan) for the CREMAs, and define the appropriate benefit-sharing arrangement. The scalability and replicability of the CREMA model will largely depend on its governance and managerial success and the financial sustainability during and after project completion.
31. The scalability of the shea plantations and the maintenance of the shea value chain will depend on the arrangements between private companies and communities with equitable shares in maintaining plantations and distribution of benefits. It will also depend on the ability of the country to ensure the architecture of the REDD-plus scheme and the capacity to negotiate valuable emission reductions to support the expected medium- and long-term financial scheme.
32. The project will establish pathways to scale landscape restoration across NSZ and engage multi-stakeholders to determine and implement the long-term vision for a climate smart shea industry in Ghana.

## 1.3 Sustainable development potential Scale: High

## 1.3.1. Environmental co-benefits

33. Restoration of landscapes with tree planting will contribute to the retention of soil moisture and the reduction of evapotranspiration, and support soil fertility. The greater number

<!-- image -->

- of trees will also improve water catchment and will contribute to the enhancement of water cycles.
34. Pollinating insects in West Africa depend on flowering trees as a source of food and shelter. When an area is cleared of most of its trees, leaving only species of economic importance, the resources available to pollinators are restricted and their populations might fall. The remaining trees could struggle to find pollinators for their flowers, in which case the fruit yield of pollinator-dependent trees would be diminished following scrub clearance. More trees will improve the ecosystems' ability to support pollination, along with the efforts of the communities to produce honey as a side business. Bees will in turn support the restoration of species diversity and the production of crop systems.
35. Biodiversity improvement will also be an important co-benefit, as more diverse trees will be planted and fires will be controlled to ensure forest and landscape regeneration. Landscape restoration will support natural habitats in allowing a resurgence of savannah forest species, an increase in animal species and the provision of food and non-timber products. Fire control will be critical to improve biodiversity.
36. If the REDD-plus scheme is implemented, it will provide further ecosystem services, allowing more forests to be conserved and protected in the country as a whole. The connectivity of natural forests is essential in view of the country's isolated remnants of forests.

## 1.3.2. Economic co-benefits

37. Improved and diversified agricultural production systems will ensure food security and will provide opportunities to connect to markets, generating revenues to achieve a better quality of life for the communities. Diversifying crop production will enable the communities to have a more stable income throughout the year.
38. The project will provide the communities with negotiating power to sell their shea kernels directly to companies, which will help to generate a stable income. However, there is a real need to ensure fair prices that allow communities to gain from the increasing profits of the shea business. There are also new possibilities to create on-farm employment and to ensure efficient value chains able to support local economies.
39. The restored forests will provide non-timber products that will in turn provide food, alternative wood fuel, medicines, building materials and craft products. Communities will be able to organize through the CREMAs and will have governing rules to protect forests and to allow quotas on the non-timber products. The equitable distribution of benefits from a restored forest landscape will therefore benefit several farmer communities that will in turn protect their forest resources.
40. The project will increase and diversify food production and will allow the communities to have a diversified source of income besides the shea business.
41. The capacity-building component will allow communities to improve the managerial and marketing skills to be able to negotiate their income from shea kernels and other products.

## 1.3.3. Social co-benefits

42. The farmers and communities around CREMAs will ensure more food security with the diversification of planting various trees besides shea trees. This will allow them to balance their diets and to sell more products, thus receiving income to support their livelihoods. At the same time communities will be more resilient to the effects of climate change as forest and agroforestry systems will be restored so as to have an effect on microclimates around the selected towns.

<!-- image -->

GCF/B.26/02/Add.09

Page 112

43. The CREMA systems will allow the communities to have a better government system and to organize themselves to take care of their own ecosystems. The model will also promote social cohesion and will support equitable benefit-sharing. Communities will also learn more about land-use planning and will be able to understand the value of avoiding deforestation.
44. The project will develop communication and information tools that will help the communities to understand the importance of the efforts made to restore and develop more efficient food systems.
45. The project will also support the involvement of woman in managing the shea cooperatives, diversifying their source of income through sustainable production options.
4. 1.3.4. Gender-sensitive development
46. Living conditions in northern Ghana are difficult for poor agricultural communities that have seen the returns from their agricultural production diminish. Women have turned to the shea business to complement their incomes, but they still face persistent gender inequalities that limit their ability to fully benefit from and participate in the country's development.
47. The production of shea has largely been carried out by women and youth. The production process is strenuous and involves heavy work. The collection of firewood used in the shea kernel processing and the money spent on fuel for the mills represent a significant human and financial cost for the local communities and shea producers. Deforestation and the loss of forest cover contribute to land degradation while smoke from the firewood represents a health hazard and contributes to greenhouse gas emissions.
48. Ghana has a Gender and REDD+ Roadmap drafted in 2011 and has included the main vision in the 2015 National REDD+ Strategy. The goal is to give men and women equal opportunities to access, participate in, contribute to and benefit from various REDD-plus policies, programmes and funds, and hence contribute to environmental and social sustainability.
49. The gender action plan was developed during a four-day consultation in August 2019, based on eight targets, including building the capacity of women and providing them with adequate resources (e.g. technology, financing, know-how) to effectively play a key role in the shea value chain. The plan also focuses on giving equal rights to women and men to control access, manage, harvest and market forest products and enjoy associated benefits within the CREMA. In addition, it addresses women's representation of at least 40 per cent in decisionmaking.
50. The gender plan is mostly oriented to training, capacity-building and inclusion. Women will be part of the fire brigades and of the workforce to build nurseries and transplant trees. However, training alone is not sufficient. There is a need to ensure that they have seed money to allow them to build their CREMA funds, to sustain their shea cooperatives and to maintain the forest landscapes to allow the theory of change of the project to happen.
51. According to the funding proposal, the expected income streams created throughout the project (through facilitating aggregation and wood-related cost savings) for 20,000 women collectors is estimated at USD 3,150,000. However, the project is investing USD 30 million and the results for female-headed households seem limited. The independent TAP wonders whether it would be more cost-effective to give those resources directly to the woman´s funds in the CREMAs, reducing some of the overestimated costs mentioned in section 1.6 below on efficiency and effectiveness.

## 1.4 Needs of the recipient Scale: High 1.4.1. Vulnerability of the country and vulnerable groups

<!-- image -->

52. Ghana has one of the most stable economies in sub-Saharan Africa and saw a period of rapid economic growth from the turn of the century to 2013 with gross domestic product (GDP) growth rates of between 5 and 6 per cent per annum. According to the World Bank, Ghana continued to expand in 2019 with a GDP estimated at 6.7 per cent in the first quarter. 2  Ghana is considered to have lower middle income status and made significant progress in the Human Development Index, moving from 0.455 in 1990 to 0.579 in 2015, which placed it in the medium human development category.
53. The country has a diversified economy driven largely by strong agricultural commodity prices and the growth of the mining sector and development of an oil and gas sector. The service sector has grown over the last 10 years, accounting for a large proportion of GDP. Growth within the agriculture sector has gradually declined and its contribution to GDP has declined to 20 per cent from 36 per cent in 2000. The sector employs more than 50 per cent of the workforce, a figure that rises to 70 per cent in NSZ. The region is the main source of shea kernels within Ghana with total production estimated to be close to 100,000 tonnes, of which 25,000 tonnes is exported, with the industry being identified as generating approximately USD 30 million in revenue annually.
54. Even though the agriculture sector in NSZ contributes to the national GDP, it is carried out using very traditional methods and rudimentary technology, and with high levels of inequality. According to the funding proposal, levels of average income could be less than one third of those of urban areas. Ghana's agriculture is predominantly smallholder, traditional and rainfed.
55. These vulnerabilities, combined with a lack of alternative income opportunities, low levels of literacy (as low at 30 per cent) and low population densities that make the provision of services difficult, have made the three regions of the north the poorest of all in Ghana with poverty rates in excess of 80 per cent in many districts and nearly 10 per cent of households being identified as food insecure.
56. The country lost over 60 per cent of its primary forest cover between 1950 and the turn of the last century (2.7 million ha). Between 2001 and 2015 the deforestation rate was 3.6 per cent per year (315,145 ha/year), driven primarily by the conversion of forest to agriculture and accounting for 66 per cent of Ghana's average annual forest sector emissions of 61.6 MtCO2eq. The main drivers of emissions are legal and illegal logging (16.9 MtCO2eq per annum, or 28 per cent of emissions), wood fuel (3.5 MtCO2eq per annum, or 6 per cent of total emissions) and forest fires (0.7 MtCO2eq per annum, or 1 per cent of total emissions).
57. Ghana is highly exposed to climate change and variability, especially in the northern part, where 35 per cent of the land mass of the country is a desert with increasing rates of desertification. As a tropical country without high altitude the country has average temperature figures ranging between 24 and 30 °C. The annual rainfall amount in Ghana has decreased over the years and has become more erratic. The feasibility analysis presents a 30-year mean of observed temperatures and precipitation in six ecological zones to predict future scenarios; average temperatures are generally expected to riseby 0.6 °C, 2.0 °C and 3.9 °C in 2020, 2050 and 2080, respectively. Rainfall levels are likely to reduce by between 1.1 and 3.1 per cent across all the agroecological zones by 2020.
58. Ghana has suffered from droughts, forest fires and floods, with rural populations in the north exposed to greater vulnerability. There is an increased risk to poor farmers as they are finding it difficult to predict the changing climate and seeing the failure of crop production. Food insecurity in the north is a major threat with 453,000 people being food insecure. In terms of water availability, only 45 per cent of the rural and 70 per cent of the urban population have access to potable drinking water in Ghana. Rainwater harvesting serves as the major source of

2  See &lt;https://www.worldbank.org/en/country/ghana/overview&gt;.

<!-- image -->

- surface water for many rural communities during the rainy season. In northern Ghana, aquifers have been located of between 10 and 60 m with an average of 27 m.
59. In this context, even though Ghana is a lower middle-income country it has a many disparities, especially in the northern part of the country, where the vast majority of the population are poor and vulnerable. These people need support from the government and private sector to ensure that they are more prepared to support their livelihoods under the threat of climate change, with better agricultural skills and the means to ensure regenerative agriculture as well as better market conditions to sell the shea kernels at favourable prices.

## 1.5 Country ownership Scale: High

## 1.5.1. Alignment with national climate strategy

60. Ghana has been an early mover on climate change, presenting its first national communication in 2000, ratifying the Kyoto Protocol in 2003 and submitting its second national communication in 2011, with a third in 2015. Agriculture, forestry and other land use is the largest source of carbon emissions in the country - 45.1 per cent in 2012 according to the third national communication.
61. The country has a National Climate Change Policy with a particular aim of minimizing the loss of carbon sinks by reducing activities that lead to the destruction of natural ecosystems, especially forest degradation and deforestation, and enhancing carbon stocks through programmes that restore degraded forests and ecosystems. Moreover, climate change has been guided by the National Climate Change Master Plan (2015-2020), which outlines projects aligned with the project proposal in terms of securing the integrity of the forests, sustainable wood fuel production, plantation development, conservation of trees through agroforestry and community-based natural resource management. The country has a National Climate Change Adaptation Strategy with an emphasis on mainstreaming disaster risk reduction and adaptive actions into programmes.
62. The National REDD+ Strategy is on a national scale with strategic actions at the subnational landscape level. The proposed project responds to the subnational jurisdictional REDD-plus programmes identified in the strategy through the Emission Reductions Programme for the Shea Landscape of the Northern Savannah Woodland (also known as the Shea Savannah Woodland Programme). The proposed project contributes to three elements of the REDD+ Strategy to: (a) significantly reduce emissions from deforestation and forest degradation, while enabling carbon stock enhancement through sustainable forest management and forest restoration; (b) transform the approaches for the production of Ghana's major agricultural commodities into climate smart production systems and landscapes; and (c) expand platforms for cross-sector and public-private collaboration and sustainable economic development.
63. The revised Forest and Wildlife Policy (2012) is the overall sector policy for conservation and sustainable development of forest and wildlife resources in Ghana. The CREMA proposed in the project derives from the CREMA designed to protect wildlife resources. Currently the government is passing a Management Bill that will enable the CREMA to be given legal status, which will formalize the role of communities.
64. Ghana has a Forestry Development Master Plan (2016-2036) that aims to achieve a just, prosperous and sustainable forestry sector that inclusively and continuously optimizes welfare and provides adequate means of livelihoods to all Ghanaians, in tandem with the 2012 Forest and Wildlife Policy. The country also has a Forest Plantation Strategy 2016-2040 that seeks to achieve a sustainable supply of planted forest goods and services to deliver a range of economic, social and environmental benefits, with five strategies aligned with the proposed project.

<!-- image -->

GCF/B.26/02/Add.09

## 1.5.2. Capacity of accredited entities and executing entities to deliver

65. The United Nations Development Programme (UNDP) will serve as the accredited entity and execution agency for the project. UNDP is one of the world's largest brokers of climate change grants for developing countries, with a current portfolio of USD 1.34 billion in mitigation and adaptation grant-financed projects in over 140 countries, supported by co-financing of USD 6.7 billion. The project builds on the experience and extensive worldwide forest programmes of UNDP, which, through a range of partnerships, currently amount to approximately USD 750 million. GCF has approved several projects, including REDD-plus results-based payment for Brazil and Ecuador, with UNDP as the accredited entity.
66. As a partner of the United Nations Programme on Reducing Emissions from Deforestation and Forest Degradation, UNDP supports indigenous peoples and forestdependent communities, and has played a leading role in supporting countries to develop national REDD-plus strategies and action plans that are inclusive and which respond to national development priorities as well as the requirements of the UNFCCC process.
67. UNDP has a team of 35 REDD-plus experts supporting more than 40 countries and has country offices supporting the development of climate change projects. The UNDP Ghana Country Office was established in 1978 and has been a trusted partner in supporting Ghana´s development process. UNDP has been playing a leading role in supporting the mainstreaming of climate change and environmental considerations into development planning, including the development of a climate change policy framework in the country.
68. The executing entity for this project is Ghana's Forestry Commission, part of the Ministry of Lands and Natural Resources. The Forestry Commission is responsible for regulating the utilization of forest and wildlife resources, including fire management and control, the conservation and management of those resources and the coordination of policies related to them. It has decentralized offices at the regional and district level. The Forestry Commission will be responsible for coordinating the implementation of the proposed project, relying on its various divisions and decentralized offices, as well as working in close collaboration with relevant stakeholders.
69. The Forestry Commission has vast experience in managing and implementing projects and programmes relating to forest land use, including biodiversity conservation, wildfire management, natural resources and environmental governance. It has managed resources above USD 50 million, for projects such as the High Forest Biodiversity Conservation Project (USD 7.8 million), the Northern Savanna Biodiversity Conservation Project (USD 8.2 million) and the Natural Resources and Environmental Governance Project (USD 40 million) with funds from a number of sources including the Global Environmental Facility (GEF) and the Wildfire Management Project (USD 12 million) funded by the Government of the Netherlands.
70. The Forestry Commission is currently implementing the Forest Investment Program being funded by the Forest Carbon Partnership Facility (USD 4.5 million) and the African Development Bank (USD 1.5 million), which is piloting key REDD-plus activities in the western and Brong-Ahafo regions of Ghana. The Forestry Commission, through the Climate Change Directorate, has completed the REDD-plus readiness phase with funding from the Forest Carbon Partnership Facility (USD 8.6 million) and signed an emission reduction purchase agreement with the Carbon Fund for the Ghana Cocoa Forest REDD+ Project (GCFRP).
71. However, this project is by far the biggest proposed investment managed by the Forestry Commission, and therefore there is a question mark on its responsibility and ability to manage this amount of resources, especially in components 1 and 2.
72. The project will work closely with the Global Shea Alliance (GSA), a non-profit industry association based in Accra with 500 members from 35 countries, including women's groups, brands and retailers, suppliers and NGOs. The Sustainable Shea Initiative, which is a USD 13

<!-- image -->

- million, five-year public-private partnership agreement to promote shea markets worldwide and improve sustainable production in Africa has 16 ongoing public-private partnerships in Ghana. Even though GSA involves sustainability as one of its principles, there is a need to make a more effective commitment to support communities in maintaining shea plantations and increasing shea kernel prices.
73. Several ministries and entities have been identified as partners of the project, but their roles will be determined during implementation. Among them are the Ministry of Food and Agriculture. which has seven directorates as well as regional and district information and monitoring officers, the Forestry Research Institute of Ghana and the Environmental Protection Agency, which is the national focal point for climate change and is responsible for the submission of all national communications to the UNFCCC secretariat as well as being the climate change data hub.
74. The Ghana Cocoa Board, which is responsible for facilitating the production, processing and marketing of good-quality cocoa, coffee and shea fruit in all forms in the most efficient and cost-effective manner, is also a partner. The Cocoa Research Institute substation in Bole will support the project with training for the on-farm trials and propagation. The Ghana Cocoa Board has developed a draft shea strategy, which will be finalized during the life of the project.
75. Other identified stakeholders include Kwame Nkrumah University of Science and Technology and NGOs such as A Rocha Ghana, World Agroforestry, Noé, Birdlife International, International Union for Conservation of Nature Ghana and Clientearth/TaylorCrabbe Initiative.
76. Finally, the project will be governed by a project board, which is responsible for making management decisions by consensus when required, including the approval of project plans and revisions. Aside from UNDP and the Forestry Commission, the board will include representatives from GSA (representing the private sector partners), representatives from civil society, research and academia, the national REDD-plus gender sub-working group and other relevant government Governmental Entities, including the Ministry of Food and Agriculture, the Ministry of Lands and Natural Resources, the Ministry of Finance, the Environmental Protection Agency and the Ministry of Environment, Science, Technology and Innovation.
77. The independent TAP believes that the Ministry of Agriculture and the Ministry of Industry should have a stronger voice in the project in order to support an integrated  food system dimension of the project and the relevance within the agro-industry development, ensuring truly equitable distribution of benefits to communities.

## 1.5.3. Engagement with civil society organizations and other relevant stakeholders

78. The project presents a summary of consultations and a stakeholder consultation plan. The first consultation was developed in April 2017 to create the Task Force for the Development of the Ghana Shea Landscape Emission Reductions Project.
79. Consultations were also developed with shea private sector actors through a series of discussions with GSA in 2017 and 2018. Early interest, particularly from the buyers and NGOs, enabled the formulation of output 2 of the project, which focused on degraded shea parklands restored through public-private partnerships, including activity 2.1 on restoration and sustainable management of shea parklands, activity 2.2 on improving sustainability of shea processing at village and industrial scales and activity 2.3 on enhancing an enabling environment for climate smart shea production.
80. These private sector consultations also led to the development of a new activity 2.2.5 on upgrading industrial processing equipment to reduce greenhouse gas emissions. The planned sub-activity will see private sector companies providing co-finance for upgrading their processing capacity in Ghana, using cleaner production techniques to avoid greenhouse gas emissions, and bringing additional socioeconomic benefits in the form of enhanced value

<!-- image -->

GCF/B.26/02/Add.09

Page 117

- addition and job creation. One key result of this private sector and NGO stakeholder engagement has been the successful inclusion in the project of co-finance from 15 separate organizations.
81. Other relevant consultations with REDD-plus stakeholders are described, with concrete recommendations on the validation of the REDD-plus scheme. Consultations were also developed with relevant ministries, including the Ministry of Food and Agriculture.
82. The stakeholder consultation plan presents the vision to develop a participatory process, to engage relevant stakeholders and to ensure the grievance redress mechanism with the model already set up for REDD-plus in Ghana.
83. However, the independent TAP considers that the project is designed with a top-down approach and should develop further consultations with communities to ensure that their voices are heard in the design and implementation of the project.

## 1.6 Efficiency and effectiveness Scale: Medium

## 1.6.1. Cost-effectiveness and efficiency

84. The total project budget is USD 54,946,775, from which USD 30,100,000 is expected to be financed by GCF, USD 15,039,622 from the Forestry Commission, USD 9,057,153 from the private sector and USD 350,000 from UNDP.
85. The expected lifetime emission reductions is 25.24 MtCO2eq, with an estimated cost of USD 2.16 per tonne of CO2eq. The estimated GCF cost per tonne of CO2eq removed is USD 1.19. This is very low, demonstrating that if future REDD-plus schemes are put in place in Ghana, there is potential to gain from emission reductions.
86. The budget expenditures involving GCF financing are heavy on (i) staff salary; (ii) international consultancy services; (iii) travel expenses; (iv) contractual services (including too many meetings with snacks); (v) equipment including vehicles and motorcycles, furniture, mobile phones for communities; and (vi) information and technology equipment and printing costs. All these expenses appear exaggerated.
87. The proposal assumes that the revenues from community management of forests such as timber, non-timber forest products and charcoal, and the sale of surplus agricultural products from the taungya groups and shea value chain will provide the start-up capital to set up or strengthen existing and proposed community funds in each CREMA. Moreover, with these resources, the communities will be expected not only to pay for the CREMA management and governance but also to fund activities such as fire management, management of areas for fuel wood, tree planting and maintenance or any other green activity to maintain the forests growing and standing.
88. The feasibility study analyses the challenges facing the CREMA model in terms of sustainability and expansion. The first challenge is financial viability, as a CREMA takes significant time and resources to establish with estimates of costs varying from USD 40,000 to 400,000. Moreover, to maintain CREMAs some chiefs have opted to destroy forests for fast cash (including rosewood cutting).
89. According to the proponents, over the past 18 months UNDP and the Secretariat have thoroughly discussed how to set up and incorporate the CREMA fund into the project and ensure that they can be established and operationalized by the time the project ends to further promote sustainability for the project and to enable the community to continue to manage the CREMA from the revenues from, inter alia, sustainable charcoal, fuel wood and non-timber forest products as per their forest management plans.

<!-- image -->

90. However, at this stage, the project is basically using the GCF grant to establish the CREMAs as well as ensure the necessary enabling conditions for this process, but the question of the funding remains.
91. It is worth mentioning that shea trees normally start fruiting after 7 years and more reliably after 15 years. The expected revenues from plantations will not materialize until the shea forests and other valuable forests are mature enough to be able to produce timber and non-timber forest products. The project initially expected that, if more advanced propagation techniques (i.e. grafting and other types of vegetative propagation and growth cultures) were used, shea trees would start fruiting much earlier than 15 years with the expectation of receiving revenues from only 2 to 4 years. However, based on comments from the independent TAP about the reality of these estimates as the techniques are only in a period of trial by research institutions, UNDP changed the proposal to acknowledge fruiting after 7 years when the project finalizes.
92. Therefore, the means to sustain vast areas of plantations by communities from years 3 to 7 (between the initial period to deliver nurseries and project completion) are rather loose, and the sustainability of the project after project completion will depend on the increased income generated by communities, mainly from shea kernels.
93. Based on three iterations of questions by the independent TAP on the value for money of the proposal and the need to ensure that more resources go to the communities rather than to all the above-mentioned expenses, UNDP came back with two minor changes: (1) they reduced the amount of the proposed grant from USD 30,500,000 to USD 30,100,000 (in other words they reduced expenses by USD 400,000); and (2) they shifted around USD 1 million in expenses (e.g. the budget of travel was above USD 5.1 million and was reduced to USD 4.19 million) and allocated some of the budget to business and enterprise development and the implementation of activities (facilitated by the local business advisory centres), logistics, travel and equipment for CREMA members to carry out thinning and forest regeneration techniques and logistics for fire brigades to conduct early burning.
94. On the other hand, the independent TAP also questioned the way in which communities would continue their business as usual model to gain resources and maintain their livelihoods from years 2 to 7 and the economic estimates of their increased production during these years. The final responses incorporated estimates by output. For example, in output 1, the benefits from wood fuel start accruing from year 2, with actual net benefits for the CREMA. Although largely fluctuating from 67,000 to 229,000 Ghanaian cedis (GHS) during the project period, these positive net benefits build up steadily from GHS 200,000 two years after the project ends to GHS 353,000 in year 20.
95. In terms of output 2, women will continue to collect kernels from existing trees in the usual way, representing about GHS 58 per woman/ha/year in revenues, increasing to GHS 75 per woman/ha/year thanks to increases in revenue from improved shea processing techniques acquired from training as well as aggregation warehouses. After year 7, when new trees begin to fruit, women will increase revenues by GHS 38 per woman/ha, building up to GHS 150 from year 12 onward.
96. Farmers engaged in MTS models will be able to accrue net benefits from intercropping from year 2 to 5, in a range of GHS 154,000 to 675,000 per farmer/ha. Year 6 and 7 will not see any benefits but a large benefit of GHS 9,883,000 is expected in year 8 from sawn timber, as well as GHS 771,000 in years 16 and 20.
97. It is difficult for the independent TAP to judge these estimates, as they depend on the effective restoration of the landscape, the number of people involved in each of the productive systems and the market links. Therefore, the suggestion is to present a much stronger economic case once the project action plan is designed with the participation of the communities.

<!-- image -->

98. In terms of the contribution by the private sector, the project present letters of cofinancing commitment from 14 companies willing to invest in cash and in kind during the 7 years of the project. From these commitments it is worth mentioning that a company called Form International is willing to put up USD 4 million. In addition, the Tungteixa Women's Association is willing to invest USD 350,000 and World Agroforestry is investing USD 755,000.
99. On the question from of the independent TAP on the effective use of these resources to support communities, the most recent budget lists activities, mainly for training and capacitybuilding for communities through NGOs, adding up to all the training already envisaged with the GCF resources. In terms of real allocations to support expenses, USD 390,000 is budgeted for warehouse construction and USD 500,000 to purchase seeds.
100. One of the annexes to the funding proposal is an operation and maintenance warehouse manual designed by GSA that presents interesting facts about the pricing of shea kernels. A warehouse cooperative is an organization of women's groups that collect shea kernels. The most important benefit for shea collectors is the improved profit per bag. By joining a cooperative and signing contracts to sell their kernels, shea collectors can increase their profit by up to 51 per cent. The assumption is that with a warehouse cooperative for 450 women, the price per bag rises from USD 15.72 to USD 23.81 and the profit per woman rises from USD 31.44 to USD 47.62. This annex is not in line with the latest responses to the economic analysis as referred to above.
101. However, assuming that women receive additional income after project completion when shea trees are already fruiting and additional income from other forest resources, the question is whether they will be willing to invest the additional income to maintain the vast agroforestry landscape, including managing fire and grazing risks, without additional support from the private sector or the government.
102. The project estimates that a USD 55.9 million investment will create income streams (through facilitating aggregation and wood-related cost savings) for 20,000 women shea collectors estimated at USD 3,150,000,53. However, regarding the question from the independent TAP on the estimated income for the private sector from the project investment, the proponents responded that 500,000 tonnes of kernels are exported from West Africa every year. Considering a price of USD 4,000 per tonne for stearin, USD 600 per tonne for olein and USD 2,000 for butter, the industry revenue is worth USD 510,000,000 for the food and cosmetic markets. According to these numbers it seems that the income to communities of the project investment is relatively small, compared with the vast revenues of the industries.
103. Since the project will end up benefiting mostly the profits of the big industries, it is fair to question why GCF grant resources are needed to support profitable shea businesses. There could be a gradual approach to using the power of the market to ensure social and environmental benefits with greater equity. This might be achieved with fair shea prices and more responsibility of the profitable shea industries in maintaining shea plantations.
104. The resources from the Government of Ghana will mostly support the plantations of the MTS models in output 3 and a minor proportion of outputs 1, 4 and 5.
105. The project expects that revenues from results-based payments from REDD-plus could sustain the activities initiated by the proposal. However, the project involves a small amount of resources to this end and the proposal does not present the true stage of advancement of the REDD-plus architecture in Ghana.
106. Overall, the project should develop a much stronger case in terms of sustainability, value for money and economic estimates to ensure that the communities are able to sustain their CREMAs, the shea cooperatives and the restored landscapes, with more precise agreements with the private sector.

<!-- image -->

GCF/B.26/02/Add.09

Page 120

## II. Overall remarks from the independent Technical Advisory Panel

107. The independent TAP recommends that the Board approve the project subject to the following condition:

- (a) Prior to the second disbursement in respect of the project, the accredited entity shall submit to the Secretariat a sustainability report, in a form and substance satisfactory to the Secretariat, which contains:
- (i) The financial sources and agreements to fund the CREMA funds during and after project completion;
- (ii) Details of agreements of the Forestry Commission of Ghana with the private sector to use their committed resources and to provide further support to sustain the shea landscapes after project completion; and
- (iii) An economic analysis of the opportunities to sustain both income generation by communities compared with accurate costs for maintaining shea plantations, modified taungya system plantations and savannah forests/woodlands to maintain sustainable productive landscapes in order to ensure that communities receive sufficient income to cover the costs plus additional profits to support better livelihoods. The analysis should propose financial options to ensure the sustainability of the project interventions after project completion. This analysis should be done in a participatory manner with communities to ensure a bottom up approach.

## Response from the accredited entity to the independent Technical Advisory Panel's assessment (FP137)

Proposal name:

Ghana Shea Landscape Emission Reductions Project

Accredited entity:

United Nations Development Programme (UNDP)

Country/(ies):

Ghana

Project/programme size:

Medium

## Impact potential

UNDP is in agreement with the ITAP assessment finding that overall, the combined mitigation outputs to restore savannah forests, shea parklands and modified Taungya system forest plantations together with the adaptation impacts have a medium-high potential impact.

The project also provides the momentum and the space for dialogue to help coordinate the current or planned wider initiatives related to national REDD+ readiness and implementation from multiple actors, which will jointly enable Ghana to benefit from results-based payments from market and non-market sources (incl. project beneficiaries through the benefit sharing arrangement supported by the project).

## Paradigm shift potential

UNDP is in general agreement with the ITAP assessment of a 'medium-high' potential.

Our shared objective is to partner very closely with communities to achieve sustainable results through a participatory and socially inclusive process. The project will provide handson, continuous training and support (training of trainers, coaching) and the means for communities to jointly manage their landscapes. The paradigm shift is realised as CREMAs, shea cooperatives and Taungya communities sustainably forge ahead with the systemic and governance changes made and the skills, options, wherewithal and physical assets that they have acquired.

## Sustainable development potential

UNDP is in agreement with the ITAP assessment finding that the sustainable development potential of the project is high.

The project will support four key conditions to reduce risks and enable transition to sustainability: (i)  clear, secure and broad rights; (ii) strong social networks; (iii) clear, enforceable rules and procedures governing forests and associated enterprises; (iv) and fair alliances between communities and their partners.

As noted by iTAP, beyond training the project to provide means and materials for shea women's cooperatives to maintain nurseries and plant trees, they will also be supported with infrastructure (warehouses) and market linkages through private sector co-financing.

## Needs of the recipient

<!-- image -->

UNDP is in agreement with the ITAP assessment finding that the project meets the needs of the recipient and the ''high'' rating provided.

## Country ownership

UNDP agrees with ITAP that the country ownership is high and also agrees that the Ministry of Agriculture has a strong voice in the project with provisions made for this.

UNDP wishes to reiterate that the project has been conceived in sustained consultation with a wide range of stakeholders including civil society, local communities and women shea collectors.

Kindly note, para 135 of the FP corrected, showing requisite experience by the Forestry Commission to manage the project. 1

Since the start of GSA's sustainability programme, income made by 385,544 participating women has increased by 45% in average. UNDP and government are both committed to ensure fair contractual arrangements between the groups and the private sector.

## Efficiency and effectiveness

UNDP is committed to ensure the value for money with 40 years' experience successfully developing and implementing projects taking into account the cultural and context specific aspects of working with communities in various landscapes. The financial and operational planning is undertaken very carefully and incorporates lessons for the need to engage very deeply and interactively with the local communities.

UNDP is also very committed, as per its own mandate, to ensure balanced relationships between the local communities and the international private sector. The partnership with GSA, for which sustainability and fairness are key components of their programme, is a cornerstone of the project and an opportunity to develop long-term, viable and mutually beneficial contractual arrangements. 2

1  The FC is currently implementing a large portion (USD 30 million) of a USD 50 million Forest Investment Program (FIP) being funded the Climate Investment Funds. which is inter alia piloting key REDD+ activities in the Western and Brong-Ahafo Regions of Ghana. The FC, through the Climate Change Directorate has completed the REDD+ Readiness Phase funded by FCPF (USD 8.6M) and signed an emissions reductions purchase agreement with the Carbon Fund for the GCFRP. The FC signed an agreement with the World Bank for USD 50 million with Government and private sector commitments.

2  A current example is a partnership between UNDP, Government of Ghana and Mondelez International to promote environmental sustainability of cocoa production As part of this project, since 2014 over 40,000 farmers have been trained via direct farmer trainings in both sustainable natural resource management practices and sustainable ecosystem management practices. More than 1,000,000 economic tree seedlings were distributed for plantings to enhance both tree and carbon stocks on cocoa farms. The initiative has piloted the establishment of two (2) Community Resource Management Area (CREMA) covering 63 communities. Field observations have shown that farmers are adopting most of the improved practices they have received training on. There is an estimated 80% adoption rate for soil improvement practices. For instance, farmers now understand and know why and how they should create buffer zones on farms with water bodies in order not to pollute them and cause harm to aquatic life and other living organisms. They are gradually moving from 'sun' cocoa to shaded cocoa through economic tree integration on their farmers; their perception on climate change and its negative effects on the environments have improved and they now know they have to farm more sustainably in order to conserve the ecosystem for future generations. Overall, it has led to increase yields (as reported by farmers) as farmers now produce more tonnage than previously resulting in enhanced incomes and therefore improved standards of living.

## 1. Introduction

This gender assessment aims to provide an overview of the situation in Ghana, with a specific focus on the GSLRP proposal. It therefore identifies gender issues that are relevant to the proposal, and examines potential gender mainstreaming opportunities. The assessment was based upon available data from studies conducted by the Government of Ghana, including the Forestry Commission, donor agencies, and multilateral development banks. Specifically, this assessment involved:

- a. Undertaking  a  desktop  review  of  existing  gender  analyses  and  gender  and  natural  resource management sector resources within Ghana;
- b. Aligning  approaches  in  this  proposal  with  the  national  priorities  of  Ghana  as  provided  in  the roadmap developed by the Forestry Commission to guide the design and implementation of a gender-sensitive REDD+ strategy;
- c. Reviewing  and  considering  national  aspirations  as  detailed  in  the  National  Policy  on  gender, women's empowerment and social protection;
- d. Interacting with stakeholders (women, men and youth) living within the shea landscape;
- e. Incorporating information and lessons learned from past studies and assessments on gender in Ghana  from  the  Government  of  Ghana,  the  United  Nations,  civil  society  organisations,  and multilateral development banks; and
- f. Integrating gender considerations in the project's indicators, targets and activities, and identifying women as leaders and decision-makers.

## 2. Brief Overview of the Gender Dynamics of the Shea Landscape

Ghana's  forestlands  are  endowed  with  rich  natural  resources  -  gold,  timber,  non-timber  forest products (NTFPs), etc. With a current rate of deforestation and forest degradation estimated at 3% annual  loss  of  forest  cover 1 ,  (320,803ha/year),  Ghana's  forest  resources  face  pressures  from agricultural  expansion,  logging,  wood  harvesting  for  charcoal  production  and  firewood,  mining, wildfires and poaching. Ghana's economic growth and achievements have come at a significant cost to its forests.  Having lost over 60% of its primary forest cover from 1950 to the turn of the last century (2.7 million hectares) 2 the future of Ghana's forests is an issue of major concern. The project landscape consists of difficult living conditions arising from forest degradation, which in turn leads to further loss of forests and forest resources. The landscape is mostly rural and the population is mainly agrarian and typically involved in small-scale subsistence and market farming as well as the male dominated cash  crop  sector.  There  is  low  population  density  and  settlements  are  scattered.  Women's participation  in  agriculture  is  particularly  higher  during  the  farming  season  and  post-harvest transportation, storage and marketing. Low soil fertility in many areas is encouraging communities to access fertile areas in forest reserves to farm. Poverty levels are high and many farmers cannot afford the cost of fertilisers and other agrochemicals. The increasing cost of agricultural inputs continues to threaten the little gains farmers make, negatively impacting household income. In response, women

1 Draft National RL submitted to UNFCCC

2 Owusu, J.G.K, Abeney, E.A, Frimpong, E.A (1999). Workshop for media personnel on forestry and wildlife reporting

I

<!-- image -->

## Annex 8 - Gender Assessment and Action Plan

GSLREP GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL

have  turned  to  the  shea  business  to  complement  their  incomes. 3 However,  Ghanaian  women, particularly in the shea landscape, continue to face persistent gender inequalities that limit their ability to fully benefit from and participate in the country's development. Women are disadvantaged relative to men in terms of access to and control over resources and services, education and training and participation in decision-making.

## 3. Existing Gender Inequality in Ghana

Although Ghana has seen an increase in its Human Development (HDI) value moving from 0.455 in 1990 to 0.579 in 2015 4 , this progress, while significant, has not been universal and disparities exist between women and women and across social groups and geographical areas. Ghana's HDI when adjusted for inequality falls to 0.391 a fall of 32.5% 5 . Many of the challenges of inequality can be seen between a growing urban population, that is benefiting from higher wages and improved access to services,  and  a  rural  population  many  of  whom  continue  to  rely  on  low  wage  and  subsistence agriculture. Levels of development also vary geographically across Ghana's 10 administrative regions with the three northern regions (Northern Region, Upper East and Upper West - the NSZ) being both the poorest in the country and experiencing the slowest growth rates. Additionally, given historical patriarchal influences and socialization processes within the home and public settings, deeply rooted gender  inequalities  still  exist  in  the  social  and  economic  system.  These  gender  inequalities disadvantage women and girls and range from inequality in access to social protection, education and skilled training to inequalities in power and decision making and in macro-economic issues including trade, industry structures and productive resources. 6

More  specifically,  the  National  Gender  Policy  (2015),  highlights  the  following  key  challenges  and hurdles within the country in achieving gender equality and the empowerment of women and girls: 7

- Inequality in access to social protection by the marginalized, vulnerable and the poor.
- Inequalities  in  the  burden  of  extreme  poverty, education,  skilled  training  gaps  and  excess maternal mortality.
- Unequal  access  to  social,  economic  power  and  justice  including  lack  of  respect  for  and inadequate protection and promotion of human rights of women and girls.
- Inequalities between women and men in sharing of power and decision making at all levels and in dealing with all kinds of conflicts, in securities and threats on women and girls.
- Inequality  in  macro-economic  issues  including  trade,  industry  structures  and  productive resources.
- Stereotyping and persistent discrimination against women and girls that manifest in negative gender relations, and value for gender roles and responsibilities with severe implication for maternal health and mortality.

Conscious efforts have been made to address the impact of these inequalities by improving the legal environment through enactment of statutes to promoting girl-child education and skill training for young women. It was in this vein that the National Gender Policy was also created in 2015 with the

GSLREP GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL

goal of mainstreaming gender equality, women's empowerment and social protection concerns within national development processes 8 (see Section 4 for more information).

However, as the consultation process of the National Gender Policy ascertained, given existing gender issues, such as the feminization of poverty, socio-cultural and traditional beliefs, inadequate basic social services, low representation of women in politics, violence against women, amongst others, there often remains barriers in achieving the targets of the Gender Policy goals. These issues and corresponding gender inequalities are discussed in more detail in the proceeding sub sections below.

## 3.1 Gender Inequality Index

Through the years, several indices have developed to quantify the concept of gender inequality.  The United  Nations  Development  Programme  uses  the  Gender  Inequality  Index  (GII)  and  Gender Development Index (GDI). 9 GII is a composite measure that shows inequality in achievement between women and men in reproductive health, empowerment and the labour market while with a measures achievement in human development in three areas: health, education, and command over economic resources.  The GDI considers the gender gaps on human development between men and women.

Ghana has a GII of 0.547 in the 2015 rankings and ranks 131st out of 188 countries assessed. The GDI value in 2015 is 0.899 with a ranking of under Group 5 indicating low equality in Human Development Index achievements between men and women.

The Global Gender Gap Index (GGGI) of the World Economic Forum examines the gap between men and  women  in  four  categories:  economic  participation  and  opportunity,  educational  attainment, health and survival; and political empowerment. 10 Out of 144 countries, Ghana rankings, based on GGGI in the year 2016, are given below 11 :

| Description                            |   Score |   Rank |
|----------------------------------------|---------|--------|
| Economic participation and opportunity |   0.805 |     10 |
| Educational attainment                 |   0.931 |    119 |
| Health and survival                    |   0.973 |     85 |
| Political empowerment                  |   0.112 |     95 |
| Gender Gap Index 2016                  |   0.705 |     59 |

* Inequality = 0.00; Equality = 1.00. Source: The Global Gender Gap Report 2014

The  Organization  for  Economic  Cooperation  and  Development  (OECD)  developed  the  Social Institutions  and  Gender  Index  (SIGI),  a  composite  index  that  scores  countries  (i.e.,  0  to  1)  on  14 indicators grouped into five sub-indices: discriminatory family code, restricted physical integrity, son bias, restricted resources and assets, and restricted civil liberties to measure the discrimination against

women in social institutions across 160 countries. The 2014 SIGI value for Ghana is 0.2988 suggesting that discrimination against women is high. 12

## 3.2 Poverty

The Ghana Living Standards Survey (GLSS) reports have provided considerable data on poverty in Ghana and the GLSS 5 and 6 have indicated that a quarter of Ghanaians are poor while less than 10% are extremely poor. The poverty is intense in the rural savanna including the shea landscape with more than a quarter of people living there being described as extremely poor. The GLSS 6 emphasizes that, overall, the dynamics of poverty in Ghana over the period 2005/06 to 2012/13 indicate that poverty is still very much a rural phenomenon and there are regional variations, with the Upper West region being the poorest, and the Northern region being the highest contributor to poverty since 2005/06 periods (Cooke et al, 2016). Both regions lie within the shea landscape. Ultimately, the poverty gap ratio is steadily declining, more quickly in the urban regions, slower in the northern rural and savannah regions and this has implications for women and men, girls and boys.

In terms of distribution of wealth by quintiles, women have a greater share of wealth in the poorest quintiles and the reverse is also true. It is also estimated that average hourly earnings of women in Ghana is 57% of that of men and that regardless of the type of employment, education, age etc., women earn lower than men. 13   Female-headed households have been flagged as of special concern since, nationally, they make up about 30% of rural households. There is increasing primary economic responsibility  and  growing  vulnerability.  However,  to  tackle  female  vulnerability  to  poverty  goes beyond female headed households and must focus on the individual. Addressing intra- household gender differentiation is crucial as a fundamental way of improving women's income and economic security (Ambala, 2006).

## 3.3 Education

The Ministry of Education, its agencies and stakeholders have put strategies in place to deal with existing and future challenges of gender in the sector. Notable among these is the Education Strategic Plan, 2010 - 2020, which has placed gender equality and women's and girl's empowerment concerns, such as girl's education, at all education levels, particularly the basic level, as well as noted the use of female role models, among others.

Gender differences in enrolment for formal education have narrowed slightly in the recent past but continue to persist particularly at higher levels. Drop-out rates for boys and literacy rates are still much lower for women than men. Investment in male education is often perceived by parents to be a more beneficial use of limited funds. The cost of education keeps rising putting many families under financial strain which is evidenced by continued high drop-out rates. The current rate for girls (46 per cent) is considerably higher than that for boys (36 per cent) at the primary level. It is however expected that the recent national policy directive launched by the government to grant free education to the high school level may improve this situation. The continuing gender imbalance in access to education limits women's access to employment and productivity. In urban areas, lack of qualifications and narrow

13 Oduro, A. D., Baah-Boateng, W. and Boakye-Yiadom, L. (2010). 'Asset Accumulation by Women in Ghana: Understanding the Process'.

Available at: http://genderassetgap.org/sites/default/files/WP4.pdf

I

<!-- image -->

range of skills limits female access to formal employment. In rural areas, lack of female education limits farm productivity: currently three quarters of female farmers have no education. Inadequate literacy and numeracy skills are also reported to limit the efficiency of female traders. Educational disadvantage may be a major barrier preventing women from responding to adjustment induced incentives (Jackson et al, 2002).

## 3.4 Political participation and decision-making

Since the promulgation of the 1992 Constitution, the country has maintained continuous progress towards strengthening multiparty democracy. The Constitution establishes the following branches with  relative  division  of  power:  The  Executive,  Council  of  State,  Legislature;  and  Judiciary.    The Affirmative Action Policy, formulated in 1998 by the Government, after the Beijing Plan of Action, sets targets of 40% representation of women at all levels of governance, on Public Boards, Commissions, Councils, Committees and Official Boards including Cabinet and Council of State.

And  although  the  Constitution  guarantees  all  persons  the  opportunity  to  participate  in  decisionmaking  at  every  level,  socio-cultural  structures  and  traditional  practices  still  hinder  women's participation in the spheres of political and public life. Even though women occupy very important positions like the Chief Justice, the overall representation of women in prominent positions is still low. Only 12.75% of legislators are women. There are only 2 women legislators out of 57 in the Northern (31), Upper East (15) and Upper West (11) regions which comprise the shea landscape. 14 At the district assembly level, district chief executives are appointed by the President of Ghana. These are political appointees and an extension of the executive branch of government. Within the Northern Region, 4 out of 26 appointees are women, in the Upper East Region, 3 out of 13 are women and in the Upper West Region, 3 out of 11 are women.

According  to  Ghana's  Millennium  Development  Goals  (MDGs)  Report  published  in  2013,  in  the Judiciary, only 29% of the Supreme Court Judges and 25% of High Court judges are women. In the Civil Service, 24% of Chief Directors are women. Further, at the local government level, only 14 of the 170 Municipal  Chief  Executives  (MCEs)  and  District  Chief  Executives  (DCEs),  representing  8.2%,  were women. In Parliament (2013), the proportion of women is 10.9%, whereas in the Sub-Saharan region the average is 22.3%. 15

There have been some initiatives targeted at promoting the participation of women in public life with the development of the Women's Manifesto starting in 2003. There have also been other activities to increase votes for female candidates to parliament, but as these statistics illustrate, in terms of real representation in political life in Ghana, participation of women remains marginal. These dynamics are due in part to the fact that, traditionally, Ghanaian women are effectively under the control or authority  of  men  (initially  their  father  or  other  male  members  of  their  kin  group  and  later  their husband) for much of their lives, although they may gain in status and influence with age. As a result, women's decision-making role in Ghana is constrained in both private and public spheres, markedly so in the northern regions.
